    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLarge language models for information extraction generate structurally valid but ontologically invalid triples---where entity types violate the ontology's domain/range constraints.\nThese violations persist across the capability spectrum and cannot be addressed by grammar-constrained decoding (which lacks cross-field type awareness) or post-hoc filtering (which discards valid predictions).\n\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that compiles information extraction ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis shows that domain/range constraints exceed context-free expressiveness, yielding a two-layer architecture: a grammar layer enforces structural validity and vocabulary membership; a semantic layer enforces type constraints at runtime through stateful masking over a dynamic entity registry.\n\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nEvaluated across three 8B-class models and two relation extraction datasets (up to 96 relations), OntoCoDec composed with LoRA fine-tuning reduces domain/range violations to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% at F1 40--46\\% ($\\pm$0.3\\,pp vs.\\ unconstrained), converting irrecoverable type errors to correctable cross-field inconsistencies (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%); beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types at 8B at ${\\sim}$1.5$\\times$ latency.\n97.6--100\\% of enforcement-removed triples are false positives.\nIn zero-shot settings, DR reductions span 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; CF increases are concentrated in hallucinated entities (58.1\\%) and decrease with scale and fine-tuning.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 13,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 13
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

{
  "parentUuid": "1b42ebf9-d604-4d63-864f-50ecf590d770",

    "content": {

        "content": "% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion\n\nWe validated OntoCoDec across 2 RE datasets, 3 model families, and event extraction (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\nThe central finding is non-additive layer interaction: grammar alone achieves 3\\% DR reduction on SciERC, semantic alone 24\\%, but their composition yields 94\\% at 8B (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results})---grammar-enforced vocabulary membership creates the clean input the semantic layer requires.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent layer interaction.}\nThis dependency weakens at scale: at 70B, the semantic layer independently achieves 84\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nWe offer a speculative hypothesis: larger models exhibit improved vocabulary calibration, assigning higher probability to ontology-valid tokens and reducing the semantic layer's dependence on grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:causal-tracing}); confirming this causal mechanism requires cross-family verification beyond our current scope.\nFallback frequency varies dramatically across architectures (0 vs.\\ 5{,}882 under LoRA+S1) without affecting enforcement (both DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.21\\%), confirming grammar is necessary but increasingly insufficient as models improve.\nConstraint enforcement is preserved under INT4 quantization (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}).\n\n\\paragraph{DR$\\leftrightarrow$CF trade-off.}\nGreedy S1 reduces DR to $\\leq$\\,0.21\\% but introduces CF (zero-shot $+$9.82\\,pp, fine-tuned $+$1.18\\,pp)---an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement.\nAmong 248 zero-shot CF violations, 58.1\\% involve hallucinated entities (absent from gold) and only 7.3\\% are true cascade errors (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); fine-tuned CF drops to $\\leq$1.4\\%.\nBeam $k{=}2$ reduces CF to 0.11\\% at 1.18$\\times$ latency (Pareto-optimal; recommended for zero-shot deployment); $k{=}4$ eliminates all violations at 8B (1 residual CF at 70B) at 1.39$\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nNet effect: total violations (DR+CF) decrease 30--51\\% in zero-shot and 26--52\\% under fine-tuned greedy S1; beam $k{=}4$ eliminates all. DR is irrecoverable while CF is amenable to post-hoc resolution (TCR; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Complementarity and deployment.}\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder internalize schema knowledge via training but lack runtime guarantees; LoRA + S1 shows the two are complementary (DR 1--2\\%\\,$\\to$\\,$\\leq$0.21\\%).\nS2 (two-pass) degrades at 70B ($-$8\\,pp R-F1), consistent with CRANE~\\cite{banerjee2025crane}; we recommend S1 as default.\n\nThe root cause---per-triple enforcement cannot coordinate cross-triple type assignments (Level~3; \\S\\ref{sec:decidability})---requires global entity-level consensus beyond online masking; beam search and TCR approximate this, with full integration as future work.\n",
        "numLines": 23,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 23
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section7_conclusion_compressed.tex"

      "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. When you are searching for a keyword or file and are not confident that you will find the right match in the first few tries use this agent to perform the search for you. (Tools: *)",
      "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task. Returns step-by-step plans, identifies critical files, and considers architectural trade-offs. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- statusline-setup: Use this agent to configure the user's Claude Code status line setting. (Tools: Read, Edit)"
    ],

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. 66	% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===
67	\clearpage
68	\section*{Limitations}
69	
70	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
71	CF is an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement: CF$\leftrightarrow$DR represents a fundamental trade-off (eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR; Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).
72	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.
73	Entity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; alternative orderings would require deferred constraint evaluation.
74	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations total) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors (28\%); schema gaps arise from training-data-derived constraints that may not cover all valid type pairs (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
75	Our empirical constraints are derived from training data type-pair co-occurrences; schema evolution (adding new entity types or relations) would require constraint regeneration, and the 0.6--1.0\% uncovered valid type pairs suggest potential over-restriction during transition periods.
76	We do not decompose CF violations into first-seen cascading errors versus legitimate type ambiguity; this decomposition requires gold entity type annotations at the mention level, which SciERC provides only at the entity level.
77	Post-hoc type consensus re-voting eliminates all CF in our experiments, at the cost of DR regression (up to $+$9.38\,pp on DocRED zero-shot; Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}); integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.
78	
79	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
80	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on two 8B-class models (full datasets) and one 70B-class model (SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
81	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified; the schema restrictiveness--enforcement difficulty relationship is based on three schemas.
82	Evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); LoRA hyperparameters were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization, which may underestimate DocRED potential.
83	WikiEvents fine-tuning is preliminary ($n{=}20$); DocRED uses the development set (test annotations unavailable).
84	All main experiments use greedy decoding; beam search compatibility is confirmed but comprehensive evaluation across all configurations remains future work (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}).
85	Composability with training-based schema-aware systems (e.g., GoLLIE) is architecturally supported but not empirically validated in this work.
86	
87	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
88	Our approach requires access to model logits, excluding API-only models; continuation masking assumes distinguishable token prefixes per type name, which may be challenged by morphologically similar types (e.g., \texttt{Protein} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Complex}; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
89	70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms enforcement is unaffected (Appendix~\ref{app:quantization}), though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.
90	Evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the mechanism is language-independent but CJK tokenization fragmentation may increase masking steps (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
91	While composability is validated with both LoRA and full fine-tuning (Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}), different PEFT methods may affect logit distributions differently, and further validation across adapter methods is warranted.
92	The two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:70b-ablation}).
93	
94	
95	% === Ethics Statement ===
96	\section*{Ethics Statement}
97	
98	This work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.
99	No human subjects were involved in the experiments.
100	The constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.
101	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.

"\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage[table]{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{amsthm}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage{pifont}\n\\usepackage{soul}\n\\usepackage{tikz}\n\\usetikzlibrary{arrows.meta,positioning,shapes.geometric,fit,backgrounds,calc}\n\\newcommand{\\TBD}[1]{\\textcolor{red}{#1}}\n\n\\title{OntoCoDec: Type-Constrained Information Extraction\\\\via Compiled Schema Constraints}\n\n\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n% === Abstract ===\n\\input{abstract}\n\n% === §1 Introduction ===\n\\input{section1_introduction_draft}\n\n% === §2 Background ===\n\\input{section2_background}\n\n% === §3 Method ===\n\\input{section3_method_draft}\n\n% === §4 Constraint Composition ===\n\\section{Constraint Composition}\n\\label{sec:composition}\n\\input{section4_composition_draft}\n\n% === §5 Experiments ===\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\input{section3_violation_landscape}\n\n\\input{section5_scierc_draft}\n\\input{section5_wikievents_draft}\n\\input{section5_runtime}\n\n% === §6 Related Work ===\n\\input{section6_related_work}\n\n% === §7 Discussion and Conclusion ===\n\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\input{section7_conclusion_compressed}\n\n% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint scope.}\nCF is an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement: CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR represents a fundamental trade-off (eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.\nEntity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; alternative orderings would require deferred constraint evaluation.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations total) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors (28\\%); schema gaps arise from training-data-derived constraints that may not cover all valid type pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nOur empirical constraints are derived from training data type-pair co-occurrences; schema evolution (adding new entity types or relations) would require constraint regeneration, and the 0.6--1.0\\% uncovered valid type pairs suggest potential over-restriction during transition periods.\nWe do not decompose CF violations into first-seen cascading errors versus legitimate type ambiguity; this decomposition requires gold entity type annotations at the mention level, which SciERC provides only at the entity level.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting eliminates all CF in our experiments, at the cost of DR regression (up to $+$9.38\\,pp on DocRED zero-shot; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}); integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on two 8B-class models (full datasets) and one 70B-class model (SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the schema restrictiveness--enforcement difficulty relationship is based on three schemas.\nEvaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); LoRA hyperparameters were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization, which may underestimate DocRED potential.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning is preliminary ($n{=}20$); DocRED uses the development set (test annotations unavailable).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; beam search compatibility is confirmed but comprehensive evaluation across all configurations remains future work (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nComposability with training-based schema-aware systems (e.g., GoLLIE) is architecturally supported but not empirically validated in this work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nOur approach requires access to model logits, excluding API-only models; continuation masking assumes distinguishable token prefixes per type name, which may be challenged by morphologically similar types (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex}; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms enforcement is unaffected (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.\nEvaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the mechanism is language-independent but CJK tokenization fragmentation may increase masking steps (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\nWhile composability is validated with both LoRA and full fine-tuning (Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}), different PEFT methods may affect logit distributions differently, and further validation across adapter methods is warranted.\nThe two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_70b_ablation}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_power_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_downstream_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_high_kappa_analysis}\n\\input{appendix_order_invariance}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\\input{appendix_wikievents_pilot}\n\\input{appendix_zs_crossmodel}\n\\input{appendix_quantization}\n\\input{appendix_gollie_comparison}\n\\input{appendix_ai_assistants}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
      "numLines": 136,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 136
    }
  },

    "oldString": "% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint scope.}\nCF is an architectural consequence of per-triple independent enforcement: CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR represents a fundamental trade-off (eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}), and its impact is model-dependent (S1 introduces CF for calibrated models but reduces it for poorly-calibrated ones).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF.\nEntity-first generation order is an architectural constraint: the grammar enforces entities before relations, ensuring the registry is populated before type checks; alternative orderings would require deferred constraint evaluation.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations total) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors (28\\%); schema gaps arise from training-data-derived constraints that may not cover all valid type pairs (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nOur empirical constraints are derived from training data type-pair co-occurrences; schema evolution (adding new entity types or relations) would require constraint regeneration, and the 0.6--1.0\\% uncovered valid type pairs suggest potential over-restriction during transition periods.\nWe do not decompose CF violations into first-seen cascading errors versus legitimate type ambiguity; this decomposition requires gold entity type annotations at the mention level, which SciERC provides only at the entity level.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting eliminates all CF in our experiments, at the cost of DR regression (up to $+$9.38\\,pp on DocRED zero-shot; Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}); integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on two 8B-class models (full datasets) and one 70B-class model (SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified; the schema restrictiveness--enforcement difficulty relationship is based on three schemas.\nEvaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); LoRA hyperparameters were tuned on SciERC and transferred to DocRED without dataset-specific optimization, which may underestimate DocRED potential.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning is preliminary ($n{=}20$); DocRED uses the development set (test annotations unavailable).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; beam search compatibility is confirmed but comprehensive evaluation across all configurations remains future work (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nComposability with training-based schema-aware systems (e.g., GoLLIE) is architecturally supported but not empirically validated in this work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nOur approach requires access to model logits, excluding API-only models; continuation masking assumes distinguishable token prefixes per type name, which may be challenged by morphologically similar types (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex}; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization; an 8B ablation confirms enforcement is unaffected (Appendix~\\ref{app:quantization}), though a direct 70B FP16 comparison exceeds our hardware capacity.\nEvaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the mechanism is language-independent but CJK tokenization fragmentation may increase masking steps (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\nWhile composability is validated with both LoRA and full fine-tuning (Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}), different PEFT methods may affect logit distributions differently, and further validation across adapter methods is warranted.\nThe two-pass strategy (S2) exhibits scale-dependent degradation: at 70B, S2 reduces R-F1 by 8.45\\,pp while 8B models show comparable S1/S2 performance, suggesting optimal constraint granularity varies with model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:70b-ablation}).\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.",

```text
1	\section{Beam Search Compatibility}
2	\label{app:beam-search}
3	
4	OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.
5	The main experiments (\S\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.
6	Here we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.
7	
8	\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}
9	Two stateful components require adaptation for beam search.
10	(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.
11	Our solution instantiates a fresh \texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).
12	(2)~The semantic layer's \texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.
13	Our solution isolates state per beam by calling \texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).
14	Both adaptations add ${\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.
15	
16	\paragraph{Results.}
17	Table~\ref{tab:beam-search} compares beam search ($k{=}4$, \texttt{early\_stopping=True}) against greedy decoding under S1 constraints across six model-dataset combinations.
18	
19	\begin{table}[h]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\caption{Beam search ($k{=}4$) vs.\ greedy decoding under S1 constraints (zero-shot).
23	SciERC: 551 documents; DocRED: matched 50-document subsets (same documents for greedy and beam); full 998-doc DocRED results in Table~\ref{tab:beam-docred-998-zs}.
24	ET = entity type, RV = relation vocabulary, DR = domain/range, CF = cross-field consistency violation rates (\%); F1 $\times$ 100.}
25	\label{tab:beam-search}
26	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
27	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccccc@{}}
28	\toprule
29	\textbf{Model / Dataset} & \textbf{Decoding} & ET & RV & DR$\,\downarrow$ & CF$\,\downarrow$ & E-F1$\,\uparrow$ & R-F1$\,\uparrow$ \\
30	\midrule
31	\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551)}
32	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\
33	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.53} & 32.00 & 8.56 \\
34	\midrule
35	\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B / DocRED (50)}
36	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 8.69 & 2.08 & 59.08 & 8.33 \\
37	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.55} & 58.64 & 9.37 \\
38	\midrule
39	\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / SciERC (551)}
40	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 0.08 & 6.56 & 23.49 & 2.54 \\
41	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.93} & 25.04 & 3.33 \\
42	\midrule
43	\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B / DocRED (50)}
44	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 1.58 & 6.85 & 55.35 & 6.12 \\
45	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0.94} & 55.62 & 9.47 \\
46	\midrule
47	\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / SciERC (551)}
48	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 13.40 & 1.27 & 27.46 & 3.67 \\
49	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & 13.79 & \textbf{0.57} & 26.66 & 4.37 \\
50	\midrule
51	\multirow{2}{*}{Mistral-7B / DocRED (50)}
52	  & Greedy      & 0 & 0 & 28.37 & 0.34 & 51.60 & 4.19 \\
53	  & Beam ($k{=}4$) & 0 & 0 & \textbf{21.37} & \textbf{0.19} & 51.78 & 5.70 \\
54	\bottomrule
55	\end{tabular}}
56	\end{table}
57	
58	\paragraph{Discussion.}
59	For calibrated models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B), beam search achieves DR=0\% across all four model-dataset combinations (both SciERC with 6 entity types, 551 documents, and DocRED with 96 relation types, 50 documents), regardless of schema complexity.
60	On SciERC, beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (0.93\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (0.08\%\,$\to$\,0\%), and substantially reduces CF (Qwen: 9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\%; Llama: 6.56\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
61	On DocRED (matched 50-document subsets), beam search eliminates DR for both Qwen (8.69\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and Llama (1.58\%\,$\to$\,0\%), with CF reductions of 74\% and 86\% respectively.
62	Llama DocRED shows the largest R-F1 gain ($+$3.4\,pp), consistent with beam search recovering valid type assignments that greedy decoding missed due to constraint fallback.
63	The CF reduction is an indirect effect: beam search reduces constraint-fallback frequency by exploring alternative token sequences that satisfy both grammar and semantic masks simultaneously, avoiding the fallback-to-grammar path that produces cross-field inconsistencies under greedy decoding.
64	
65	Mistral-7B-v0.3 constitutes a boundary condition on both datasets.
66	On SciERC (6 entity types), beam search does not reduce DR (13.4\%\,$\to$\,13.8\%), though CF drops by 55\% relative (1.27\%\,$\to$\,0.57\%) and R-F1 improves by $+$0.7\,pp; the unchanged DR confirms that all residual violations stem from entity type misclassification, not constraint-search failure.
67	On DocRED (96 relation types), beam search reduces DR by 25\% relative (28.4\%\,$\to$\,21.4\%): the more complex schema introduces constraint-search failures that beam exploration resolves, but the entity misclassification component remains.
68	This contrast reveals that beam search resolves constraint-search failure (where greedy decoding encounters $G(t) \cap S(t) = \emptyset$ conflicts) but cannot overcome fundamental model calibration limitations in entity typing.
69	
70	\paragraph{Beam width ablation.}
71	Table~\ref{tab:beam-ablation} varies beam width $k \in \{1,2,4,8\}$ on Qwen3-8B / SciERC (551 documents) under S1 constraints.
72	
73	\begin{table}[h]
74	\centering
75	\small
76	\caption{Effect of beam width on constraint satisfaction and extraction quality (Qwen3-8B, SciERC, 551 documents, S1 constraints). $k{=}1$ corresponds to greedy decoding.}
77	\label{tab:beam-ablation}
78	\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccc@{}}
79	\toprule
80	$k$ & DR$\,\downarrow$ & CF$\,\downarrow$ & E-F1$\,\uparrow$ & R-F1$\,\uparrow$ \\
81	\midrule
82	1 (greedy) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 30.98 & 8.36 \\
83	2          & 0.35 & 4.12 & 31.54 & \textbf{8.75} \\
84	4          & \textbf{0}    & 0.53 & 32.00 & 8.56 \\
85	8          & \textbf{0}    & \textbf{0.08} & \textbf{32.18} & 8.39 \\
86	\bottomrule
87	\end{tabular}
88	\end{table}
89	
90	DR elimination at $k{\geq}4$ confirms that constraint-search failure is resolved with moderate exploration width.
91	CF continues to decrease with larger beam (0.08\% at $k{=}8$, only 2 residual violations), while entity extraction quality improves monotonically.
92	$k{=}4$ represents the optimal cost-effectiveness trade-off: it eliminates all DR violations at roughly half the compute cost of $k{=}8$.
93	
94	\paragraph{Beam width--latency Pareto analysis (LoRA).}
95	% exp_id: d066_beam_sweep
96	Figure~\ref{fig:beam-pareto} and Table~\ref{tab:beam-sweep} extend the beam width analysis to LoRA fine-tuning, where the constrained--unconstrained interaction shifts qualitatively: LoRA + S1 already achieves DR=0\% at $k{=}1$ (greedy), so beam search targets CF reduction rather than DR elimination (contrast: zero-shot requires $k{\geq}4$ for DR=0\%).
97	Among constrained configurations, $k{=}2$ is Pareto optimal (R-F1 44.60, CF 0.11\%, 1.39$\times$ vs.\ greedy unconstrained; 1.18$\times$ vs.\ greedy S1); $k{\geq}4$ yields diminishing F1 returns with increasing latency (1.63--2.03$\times$).
98	
99	\begin{figure*}[ht]
100	\centering
101	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/beam_pareto.pdf}
102	\caption{Beam width vs.\ R-F1 under LoRA fine-tuning. Left: SciERC (Qwen3-8B solid, Llama-3.1-8B dashed; 551 docs). Right: DocRED (Llama-3.1-8B, 200 docs). Bubble area $\propto$ total violation rate (DR+CF+RV); green rings mark zero-violation configurations. Star: Pareto-optimal ($k{=}2$).}
103	\label{fig:beam-pareto}
104	\end{figure*}
105	
106	\begin{table}[h]
107	\centering
108	\small
109	\caption{Beam width sweep on SciERC (Qwen3-8B LoRA, 551 documents). \textsc{unc}: unconstrained; \textsc{con}: schema-constrained. Latency relative to $k{=}1$ unconstrained. Highlighted row: Pareto-optimal configuration.}
110	\label{tab:beam-sweep}
111	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

_source line 1248 · 2026-05-24T13:30:58.475Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Looking at page 3, Figure 1 is at the top but it looks the same size as before - the `keepaspectratio` means the width is the binding constraint (textwidth), so increasing height from 4.8cm to 5.5cm doesn't change anything if the aspect ratio already makes the figure shorter than 5.5cm at full width.

The PaperBanana image is landscape-oriented (wider than tall), so `width=\textwidth` is the binding constraint and `height=5.5cm` doesn't help. To make the figure bigger, I need to either:


1. Remove the height constraint entirely and just use `width=\textwidth`
2. Use `width=\textwidth` without keepaspectratio (would distort)

Actually, looking at it more carefully, the figure IS using `keepaspectratio` with `width=\textwidth` and `height=5.5cm`. Since it's a wide landscape figure, the width constraint will determine the size and the figure won't exceed the height anyway. The 4.8cm constraint was originally added to LIMIT the height (prevent overflow), not to enlarge it. So removing the height constraint should let the figure display at its natural size with full width, and I need to check what that looks like.

The user wants it larger, so I should examine the actual PNG dimensions to understand what's happening.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The image is 2752x1536 pixels, aspect ratio 1.79:1 (landscape). At `\textwidth` (which is about 16cm for ACL two-column), the natural height would be about 16/1.79 = 8.9cm. So the 4.8cm height constraint IS binding - it's scaling the figure down significantly. Changing to 5.5cm should have made it bigger, but let me check...

Wait, but `\textwidth` in ACL format is the full page width (for `figure*`), which is about 16cm. 

So if the height constraint is binding at 4.8cm, that means the width gets squeezed down to about 8.6cm instead of the full 16cm available. Increasing the height to 5.5cm should expand it to roughly 9.8cm wide, but looking back at page 3, the figure still appears quite small. Let me take another look at what's actually rendering.

The PDF conversion might be the culprit here—when PIL converts the PNG to PDF, it sets the page dimensions based on DPI, which could make the PDF page itself quite small. That would mean `width=\textwidth` scales it up, but the height constraint caps it back down. I should try removing the height constraint entirely or bumping it up to something like 6.5cm to see if that lets the figure expand properly. I'm settling on 6cm as a reasonable compromise—it's noticeably larger than the current size without risking overflow issues on the later pages.
```
